### Important distinction: what the audit has not yet done

This is a first-pass extraction of rows found in the online text, not completion of Segment 13's 29-row worksheet. No row should yet be upgraded to CONFIRM. The current status for the entries above is HARBOU ONLY because the underlying parish, marriage, probate and military records have not been independently read. Some personal-number cells are blank in Harbou's text; do not infer a number from a neighbouring generation.

The online text's introductory diagram is a family-history overview, not independent proof of every medieval connection. Harbou's article itself says that he is supplementing the German family history using Danish sources; it should not be used as primary proof for the earlier Pomeranian generations.

## Gaardsted — archive-series correction

The online alphabetical extract for Rolsøgård gods G302 identifies the relevant 1787 entry as:

- Niels Jensen Degn in Vrinners, probate no. 11, 14 May 1787, folio 39.
- The transcription names wife Maren Lauridsdatter, children and stepchildren, then reads: “Arv i boet til afdødes stedbørn efter Christen Christensen Tohde? foged i Dejret (skifte 1764) til datter Karen Christensdatter LV: broder Claus Gaardsted forpagter på Vosnæsgård.”

Source: https://slaegt.dk/media/jnnnvuml/alfabetisk-navneliste-og-skifteuddrag-til-rols%C3%B8g%C3%A5rd-gods-g302-mols-herred-1778-1816-1818.pdf

The extract is strong evidence that the 1787 estate mentions Karen Christensdatter as a stepchild/heir associated with an earlier probate for a bailiff named Christen Christensen “Tohde?” in Dejret, and that Claus Gaardsted is described as her brother and legal guardian, tenant at Vosnæsgård. It does **not** by itself resolve the uncertain surname reading or establish that Christen was Claus's father. The original 1787 folio still needs to be read.

A significant archive-series point: the G302 index is explicitly for Rolsøgård probate material **1778–1816 (1818)**. The cited 1764 probate therefore cannot simply be assumed to be in this same indexed series. Rigsarkivet's guidance says that before 1793 probate jurisdiction for non-freeholding rural residents depended on the estate/tenure: estate owners handled the probate of their tenants and employees; county-level probate series handled rural non-tenants. The correct 1764 archive creator must be identified from Christen's tenure/employer and the contemporary estate boundaries, rather than guessing “Rolsøgård 1764.”

Rigsarkivet guidance: https://www.rigsarkivet.dk/vejledning/skifter-og-testamenter/skiftearkivalier-kom-godt-i-gang/

Next step: inspect original G302 probate no.11, fol.39, then identify the 1764 probate's actual archive creator from the original reference and Dejret estate/tenancy context. Preserve “Tohde?” as uncertain until the original is read.

A probate's internal family relationships and the separate identity match to existing person `a66` are two different questions. No relationship or person should be attached to `a66` yet.

## Von Kleist / Muttrin — additional source comparison

A second, more important source pass checked the 2025 Muttrin volume against the family association's 2021/2022 *Urkundenbuch* edition.

### 1364: Prissebur of Muttrin is a real documentary reference

The 2025 Muttrin volume, printed pages 5–6, explicitly says that the second son of Klest (M.1) is mentioned in Urkunde 83 of 1364 as a co-owner of land at Glineke: “Prißebur, der Klest heißt von Muttrin.” It then says the names Jarßlaff and Henning as Prissebur's sons come from an early family genealogy of 1576 and an older letter in which Prissebur acted for himself and his two sons concerning Glineke.

This is not the same as the acquisition of Damen around 1365. The earlier correction in Segment 1 of the extension that said there was no 1364 reference was wrong as a blanket statement. The 1364 reference is present in the Muttrin volume; the c.1365 date concerns Damen.

The 2025 authors expressly qualify the early pedigree: Quandt's arrangement uses individual contemporary documents together with family records made considerably later, and is a *wahrscheinliche Anordnung* (probable arrangement). Accordingly:
- Prissebur's appearance in 1364 is a documented clue reported by the modern family-history edition, with the original Urkunde 83 still to be inspected.
- The parent link from Klest to Prissebur and the sons Jarßlaff/Henning have a different evidentiary basis (later family genealogy / older letter as reported by the edition).
- Later generations should be checked against each cited document rather than assigned the same confidence as the 1364 name occurrence.

Source: https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Muttrin/Muttrin.pdf, printed pp. 5–6, lines 293–320 in the accessible text.

### 1477: the collective enfeoffment gives an independent structural anchor

The 2021/2022 *Urkundenbuch*, Urkunde 125, dated Belgard 13 April 1477, reproduces the text of Duke Bugislaw X's collective fief and common-hand letter. The edition says it is based on a 16th-century authenticated copy in the Landesarchiv (Tit. IX. Sect. 88. Nr. I. fol. 1), with further transsumpts/retranssumpts listed in note 128.

The charter names:
- Bisprowe at Muttrin with sons Hennige, Petern and Hanse;
- Dubbeszlaff at Damen with sons Drewese, Curde, Bisprowe, Petern and Pribbetzlaue;
- Pribbeslaff at Muttrin with sons Reymer and Bartes;
- brothers at Tychow and Dubberow, and the Vilnow and Raddatz representatives.

The charter itself says the Kleists present testified that they descended from one “Older Vader” who had three sons, from whom came the Muttrin-Damen, Tychow-Dubberow, and Vilnow-Raddatz branches. The edition's explanatory footnotes assign genealogical numbers to some people, but those are editorial identifications and must be kept distinct from the medieval wording itself.

This is valuable evidence for the existence and structure of the three named branches by 1477. It does not independently prove every earlier generation back to 1364, and it does not by itself connect the 1477 men to Cartz Ulrik or the Danish Gedde branch.

Source: https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Gesamt.pdf, Urkunde 125, printed pp. 73–74 / accessible lines 2760–2782.

### 1402–1439 bridge records reported by the 2025 Muttrin volume

The same 2025 introduction identifies further cited records:
- Urkunde 100, 2 February 1407: Kleists act as guarantors for a cousin; named men include Kurt Klest von Damen, Hennyngh Clest von Bruskow and Hennyngh Clest von Mutteryn.
- Urkunde 92, 1389: Pritzbor appears as a cleric at Zatkow.
- Urkunden 93–95, 1402: young Bispraw Klest appears among the regional vassals/arbitrators and as a witness.
- Urkunde 105, 18 August 1439: Hermen and Jurjen Klest, sons of Curt (M.4), are listed in an agreement.
- Urkunde 104, 1435: Henning (M.8) was still living.

These references are a practical generation-by-generation audit path. They are still secondary descriptions until the underlying charter texts and identities are checked.

### Numbering warning

The 1477 Urkundenbuch notes explain that genealogical numbering begins around this charter and that the people named there form the heads of the respective branches. The 2025 Muttrin volume also uses its own M. numbering in its early Stammtafel, while the extension worksheet uses III. numbers and Harbou's Danish article uses family-history numbers. Do not assume M.1, III.281, and a similarly named person in a user-supplied Geneanet reconstruction are interchangeable. The five-number sequence III.22 → III.54 → III.114 → III.186 → III.281 remains an audit target, not an accepted chain, until every numbered biography is located and matched.

### What is now usable in the tree's research narrative

The public site may accurately state:
1. Prißebur/Prissebur is reported in the 2025 Muttrin volume as named in a 1364 document as a Muttrin land co-owner.
2. The 1477 collective fief letter records three main Kleist branches and names their representatives and sons.
3. The early generations between those anchors are partly reconstructed from later family records and selected contemporary documents.
4. The Danish descent from Cartz Ulrik / Pribislaff is a separate later chain requiring its own source reconciliation.

It should not state that the entire medieval-to-Danish pedigree is proven, or that Prissebur acquired Damen in 1364.

No canonical people, relationships, or GEDCOM records were changed by this research pass.


## Christian von Kleist II.60 (d.1679) — Harbou checked against the new Z.ai lead

The relevant passage in H. W. Harbou, “Slægten von Kleist i Danmark,” *Personalhistorisk Tidsskrift* 1897, p. 97 in the printed article / page 2 in the PDF text, has now been reread directly.

Harbou states that the Brandenburg colonel Christian von Kleist (II.60) of Groß-Tychow:
- inherited one part of the estate from his father;
- received another part with his first wife Hedwig Maria von Kleist, daughter of Georg of Groß-Tychow and Christina von Woyten;
- married second Barbara Eleonore von Kalckstein;
- died in 1679, after which his four sons from the first marriage divided the Pomeranian estates on 1 June; there were also one daughter from the first marriage and one son and one daughter from the second.

Harbou does **not** name Christian II.60's father in this paragraph. He separately says that Swedish cavalry captain Joachim Henning von Kleist (II.59) of Groß-Tychow was Christian's *Farbrodersøn*. This Danish kinship term denotes a paternal uncle's son / cousin relationship; it does not mean half-brother. Harbou therefore does not establish the proposed sequence Christian II.60 ← Bernd II.39 ← Carsten II.21 ← Joachim II.22.

The Z.ai-supplied candidate sequence:
- Christian II.60 ← Bernd II.39 + Elisabeth von Manteuffel;
- Bernd II.39 ← Carsten II.21 “the Elder” + Hedwig von Heydebreck;
- Carsten II.21 ← Joachim II.22;
- further ancestry to Bartez the Younger and Reimer II.2;

is retained as a **new unverified lead**, not a finding. It was not located in Harbou's 1897 Danish article in this pass (searches for “Bernd (II.39)” and “Joachim (II.22)” return no matching text). The next required action is to open the exact numbered biographies II.60, II.39, II.21 and II.22 in the relevant German *Geschichte des Geschlechts von Kleist* / family-history volume, inspect the original printed page images and references, then trace each parent link to its cited contemporary record. The family-history numbering is not interchangeable with the III. numbering used in Harbou's Muttrin section.

### Current evidence status

- Christian II.60's father: **not named by Harbou in the cited passage**.
- Christian II.60's first wife and her parents: **explicitly named by Harbou**.
- Christian II.60's second wife: **explicitly named by Harbou**.
- Joachim Henning II.59: **explicitly called Farbrodersøn of Christian II.60 by Harbou**; do not describe him as a half-brother.
- Bernd II.39 / Carsten II.21 / Joachim II.22 proposed chain: **unverified lead from user-supplied Z.ai notes**.
- Bartez / Reimer ancestry and Krag / Rosenkrantz expansions: **not yet source-inspected here**; do not import.

### Site correction

The site template now includes a dated bulletin item and research card explaining that Harbou does not name Christian II.60's father in this passage, that Joachim Henning II.59 is described as *Farbrodersøn*, and that the II.39 → II.21 → II.22 chain remains under verification. No family-tree or GEDCOM relationships were changed.


## Tychow-Dubberow chain: direct check of the 2025 numbered biographies

Source inspected: *History of the Family v. Kleist, Third Part, Second Department: Tychow-Dubberower line*, 2nd revised edition, German 2025; English machine translation 2026:
https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/english/Dubberow_translation.pdf
The translation's own notice says it is machine translated, not yet proofread, and is based on the 2025 German edition. Use the German original for fine wording and all legal/kinship terminology before final publication.

### The key correction to the Z.ai chain

The numbered biographies do **not** give the sequence Reimer II.2 → Bartez II.6 → Andreas II.11. They show two brothers and two separate son-lines:

- Hans of Tychow (I.24) had sons Reimer II.2 and Bartes II.3 (printed pp. 121–122 / PDF pp. 121–122).
- Reimer II.2 (born c.1425) and his wife, a von Born of the Grassee house, had three sons: Bartez II.6, Tessen II.7 and Joachim II.8. Bartez II.6 died c.1527 and married a von Zozenow; their six sons are listed as II.15–20 (printed pp. 122–123 / PDF pp. 122–123).
- Bartes II.3 was Reimer's brother. Bartes and his wife from the von Briesen family had three sons: Martin II.9, Jacob II.10 and Andreas (Drews) II.11 (printed p.122 / PDF pp.122–123).
- Andreas II.11 († after 1546) was the father of Carsten II.21 and Joachim II.22 (printed pp.132–133 / PDF pp.132–133).
- Carsten II.21 († before 1618), called “the Elder” to distinguish him from his cousin Carsten II.19, was married first to a von Hechthausen and around 1575 to his niece Hedwig von Heydebreck. The volume lists eight sons, including Bernhard II.39 (printed pp.133–134 / PDF pp.133–134).
- Bernd/Bernhard II.39 († after 1665), sixth son of Carsten II.21, was married to Elisabeth von Manteuffel of Pribbernow and Broitz. The biography says she bore him Christian II.60 and Andreas Joachim II.61 (printed pp.134–135 / PDF pp.134–135).
- Christian II.60 (†1679) is explicitly introduced as a son of Bernd II.39. The biography names his two wives: first Hedwig Maria von Kleist, daughter of Georg II.55; second Barbara Eleonora von Kalkstein. It lists four sons and one daughter from the first marriage and one son and one daughter from the second (printed pp.142–145 / PDF pp.143–145).
- Christian Casimir II.82 (born 1654, died 19 Feb 1722) is explicitly described as Christian II.60's second son. His wife was Anna Hedwig von Fürst; their daughter Barbara Juliane married Lieutenant Colonel Cartz Ulrich von Kleist (III.369) (printed pp.145–146 / PDF pp.146–147).

Thus the supported paternal branch is:
Hans I.24 → Bartes II.3 → Andreas (Drews) II.11 → Carsten II.21 → Bernd/Bernhard II.39 → Christian II.60 → Christian Casimir II.82 → Barbara Juliane → marriage to Cartz Ulrich III.369.

Reimer II.2 is the brother of Bartes II.3, not the direct father of Andreas II.11. Bartez II.6 is Reimer's son and belongs to a collateral branch. The two lines share the earlier Hans I.24 ancestor, but Bartez II.6 must not be inserted between Reimer II.2 and Andreas II.11.

### Evidence character and cautions

This is a meaningful advance: the revised family-history volume explicitly states each parent-child relationship in the numbered biographies and cites older feudal letters, investitures, tax registers, family records and other archive materials. It is still a modern secondary genealogical compilation, not a substitute for independently inspecting each cited original record. The volume itself distinguishes documentary support from family-table claims in places; for example, it reports the marriage of Reimer II.2 to a von Born based on family records, and Carsten II.21's marriages according to von der Osten. Keep the source type visible per link.

The English PDF labels itself a machine translation made in 2026 and says it has not yet been proofread. The German original should be checked for exact terms, especially “Nichte” and “Farbrodersohn,” and for any translation ambiguity.

### What is and is not now established from this pass

- Parent chain Hans I.24 → Bartes II.3 → Andreas II.11 → Carsten II.21 → Bernd II.39 → Christian II.60: explicitly stated in the revised numbered biographies.
- Reimer II.2 → Bartez II.6: explicitly stated, but this is a collateral line, not the line through Andreas II.11.
- Christian II.60 → Christian Casimir II.82 → Barbara Juliane: explicitly stated.
- Barbara Juliane → Cartz Ulrich III.369: marriage explicitly stated.
- Cartz Ulrich → Christian Adam → Christian Frederik → Frederikke Louise Dorthea → Ove Samuel Gedde → Ove Frederik Christopher Gedde: not checked in the Dubberow passages above; retain earlier Harbou/other-source audit and verify each link separately.
- Further Rosenkrantz and Krag ancestry supplied by Z.ai: not inspected in this source pass.
- No canonical GEDCOM/tree relationships have been changed. The site research card was corrected to show the two separate Tychow branches and the verified parent links, while keeping the source-quality caveat.


## Correction — Tychow/Reimer/Bartes father is disputed

A further source comparison requires qualification of the preceding Tychow-Dubberow section. The 2025 Tychow-Dubberow volume states that Reimer II.2 and Bartes II.3 were sons of Hans of Tychow I.24. However, L. Quandt's *Allgemeine Geschichte* transcribes the 13 April 1477 collective fief letter as naming “Pribbeslaw zu Muttrin mit seinen Söhnen, die Brüder Reymer und Bartes zu [Groß-]Tichow” (accessible text around line 1042). The same general-history discussion says that the name of the earlier Tychow/Dubberow branch ancestor is not known from the older family records (around lines 1373 and 1390).

These statements conflict. The 1477 transcription appears to name Pribbeslaw of Muttrin as father; the later Tychow genealogy assigns the brothers to Hans I.24. Until the original charter/authenticated copy and the genealogical reasoning are checked, the father of Reimer and Bartes must remain unresolved. The descent from Bartes II.3 to Andreas II.11, Carsten II.21, Bernd II.39 and Christian II.60 remains explicitly stated in the 2025 Tychow-Dubberow biographies. Bartez II.6 is Reimer II.2's son and is not the father of Andreas II.11.

This also means the earlier statement that the whole chain begins with Hans I.24 was too definite. The corrected source audit is in leads/von-kleist-source-audit-2026-11-16.md.

## Von Kleist additions exp020–exp041 — source audit completed, import held

A new itemized source audit has been added at leads/von-kleist-source-audit-2026-11-16.md. It distinguishes the 1364 Prissebur reference from later pedigree reconstruction; records what the 2025 Muttrin volume says about the early generations; audits all 23 proposed people exp020–exp042; and flags the structured-data mismatch where exp033's note names existing p3472 as his son but the JSON leaves p3472's father blank.

No canonical family data or GEDCOM records were changed. The next work is to locate the exact Muttrin biography for III.281/p3472, reconcile old and revised numbering, inspect the original 1477 charter or authenticated copy, and then determine which specific relationships are strong enough for a carefully scoped canonical update.


## Expanded research export repair — missing Henning M.8 generation

A structural error was found while comparing the notes with the relationships in the expanded JSON and GEDCOM:

- exp022 is Henning M.3; his note says he was father of Henning M.8.
- The Muttrin volume says Bisprow III.1 (exp024) was son of Henning M.8, not Henning M.3.
- The unnamed von Wedelstett (exp038) is the wife of Henning M.8, not Henning M.3.
- The original structured export had skipped Henning M.8: exp024.father pointed directly to exp022, and exp038.spouse pointed to exp022.

I added exp042, Henning von Mutteryn (M.8), to the **research-only expanded package** and corrected the links to exp022 → exp042 → exp024, with exp038 as exp042's spouse and exp024's mother. The same repair was applied to:
- leads/family-tree-data-expanded.json
- leads/family-tree-expanded.ged
- leads/family-tree-full-export.txt
- leads/family-tree-expanded.txt
- leads/README.md
- leads/von-kleist-source-audit-2026-11-16.md

The expanded package now has 524 people (489 original site records + 35 research additions) and 160 families. The canonical site JSON and canonical family-tree.ged remain unchanged. The p3472 ← exp033 relationship is still stated in the research narrative but is not yet encoded in structured parent fields; it remains open pending exact biography/page verification.

# Archived file: leads/review-expanded-family-tree-2026-11-16.md

# Review of expanded family tree file — 16 November 2026

## File inspected

- `leads/family-tree-expanded.txt`
- Repository version: 103,941 bytes in GitHub metadata; fetched text 98,416 characters / 1,302 lines.
- SHA: `2daeaca32eea2e0466abdb68a81bf79263464cf8`
- The file is preserved unchanged. This review is separate; no canonical family data or GEDCOM was changed.

## Overall assessment

This is a substantial research compilation, not merely a prose summary. It contains a large expanded transcription of branches and a separate November 2026 research summary. It is valuable as a working source register and clue map. It should **not** be imported wholesale or described as 39 independently verified new ancestors: the file mixes archival references, modern family-history compilations, online genealogy databases, family knowledge and hypotheses. Its own legend distinguishes some of these categories, but the summary headings sometimes overstate certainty.

The current `data/family.json` has 489 person records and 489 unique IDs (98 living/private records). The expanded file correctly says its starting tree has 489 people; its claimed 528 total is a projection for the expanded text, not the current canonical dataset.

## Critical correction: von Kleist Tychow branch is still mis-nested in this file

The file's tree section around lines 444–463 and its summary around lines 1170–1208 both give:

`Reimer II.2 → Bartez II.6 → Andreas (Drews) II.11 → Carsten II.21 → Bernd II.39 → Christian II.60`

That parentage is not what the numbered biographies in the cited 2025 Tychow-Dubberow volume state. The same volume distinguishes the two brothers and their branches:

- The 2025 Tychow-Dubberow volume says Hans of Tychow I.24 had sons Reimer II.2 and Bartes II.3.
- However, L. Quandt's transcription of the 13 April 1477 collective fief letter in *Allgemeine Geschichte* names “Pribbeslaw zu Muttrin mit seinen Söhnen, die Brüder Reymer und Bartes zu [Groß-]Tichow”. The same discussion says the earlier Tychow/Dubberow branch ancestor is not known from the older family records. The father of Reimer and Bartes is therefore disputed between the 1477 charter transcription and the later Tychow genealogy; do not state Hans I.24 as proven.
- Reimer II.2's sons include Bartez II.6.
- Bartes II.3's sons include Andreas (Drews) II.11.
- Andreas II.11 is the father of Carsten II.21; Carsten is father of Bernd II.39; Bernd is father of Christian II.60.

Therefore Bartez II.6 is a collateral relative of Andreas II.11, not his father. The expanded file's indentation and its “verified chain” paragraph both need correction before this text is reused. From Bartes onward, the branch is **Bartes II.3 → Andreas II.11 → Carsten II.21 → Bernd II.39 → Christian II.60**. Reimer II.2 → Bartez II.6 is a separate sibling branch. The parent of Reimer and Bartes remains disputed: the Tychow-Dubberow genealogy says Hans I.24, while the 1477 charter transcription in *Allgemeine Geschichte* says Pribbeslaw of Muttrin. The English PDF is a machine translation; consult the German originals and underlying charter before publication.

The file also says “five generations” were added from Reimer to Christian, but the line actually mixes the collateral Reimer/Bartez branch with the Andreas/Carsten branch. Do not repeat that generation count without recalculating it from the corrected branch.

## Other high-priority source-quality issues

### Rosenkrantz and Krag

The file labels these lines “verified,” but much of the cited support is WikiTree, Geni, Wikipedia, Finn Holbek and family-tree compilations. Those are useful discovery tools, not primary sources. The biographical dictionary may support particular public-figure facts, but it does not automatically prove every parent-child link in the chain. Treat these as promising secondary/tertiary leads until each link is backed by contemporary records or a reputable scholarly genealogy with transparent citations.

### Gedde

- The file correctly labels some Samuel Christoph / Hans Christopher Gedde relationships as uncertain, but other entries appear in the expanded tree with record symbols despite citations to modern biographies or Geni.
- The Ove Samuel Gedde birth-date conflict (1778 in Harbou vs 1788 in Anker) remains unresolved. Do not create a second Ove or overwrite the existing record on this file alone.
- Keep the 1895 family group photograph and other family-held material as separate image/source leads; the text's existence does not itself supply the actual image or permission to publish it.

### Other branches

The expanded file includes many useful indexed record references (LL/AO/DFS/ARK identifiers), but a citation identifier alone is not an extracted record. For each new relationship, retain the actual image/transcription, parish/archive, year, event and exact wording. The notes on the Gaardsted, Vibygård, Lorenz/Wilhelm Krogh, Dybbøl, Langeland and other open lines should remain leads until the cited entries have been inspected and identity matches tested.

## Data and editorial checks

- The expanded file is a separate `.txt` compilation; it does not itself update `data/family.json` or the GEDCOM.
- It reports 39 new verified entries and 528 total entries, but the actual canonical `data/family.json` remains 489 people. The claimed 39 additions need an itemized ID-to-source audit before any import.
- The file's own “verified” label is not an independent evidence grade. Reclassify each new person/relationship by the actual source type and what the source explicitly states.
- Keep biological, legal, step/social, adoptive and household relationships distinct.
- Preserve the existing raw file unchanged and record corrections in a separate review.

## Recommended next steps

1. Correct the two Tychow diagrams/summary paragraphs so Reimer/Bartez and Bartes/Andreas are shown as collateral branches.
2. Build an itemized register of the claimed 39 additions: proposed ID, name, relationship, source/page/record, exact supporting statement, evidence class, confidence and unresolved conflicts.
3. Audit Rosenkrantz and Krag links first against the cited biographical works and contemporary records; demote unsupported WikiTree/Geni-only links to leads.
4. Resolve Ove Samuel Gedde's 1778/1788 conflict independently.
5. Inspect the strongest LL/AO/DFS record references for the other branches and log negative searches with exact scope.
6. Only then prepare a small, sourced patch for `data/family.json` and GEDCOM. No structural import has been made in this review.


## Subsequent source qualification — 1477 father attribution

The statement above that Hans I.24 is the father of Reimer II.2 and Bartes II.3 is not settled. A later comparison found that *Allgemeine Geschichte* transcribes the 1477 collective fief letter as naming Pribbeslaw of Muttrin with sons Reimer and Bartes. The 2025 Tychow-Dubberow volume instead assigns the brothers to Hans I.24. This conflict is recorded in the new itemized audit at leads/von-kleist-source-audit-2026-11-16.md. Until the charter and the later genealogical reconstruction are reconciled, show the parent as disputed. The corrected chain from Bartes II.3 through Andreas II.11, Carsten II.21, Bernd II.39 and Christian II.60 remains source-stated; Bartez II.6 remains on Reimer's separate branch.

# Archived file: leads/review-new-extension-files-2026-11.md

# Review of new research-extension files

Reviewed: 16 November 2026

## Files reviewed

The following seven new files were present in `leads/`:
- `file-vx6.file` — research-extension overview, confidence/source key, and Line 1 (von Kleist / Muttrin)
- `file-5fb.file` — Line 2 (Gedde / Gjedde)
- `file-rag.file` — Line 3 (Gaardsted)
- `file-xdx.file` — Line 4 (Syv / Vibygård and Rosmus)
- `file-9cd.file` — Line 5 (Langeland / Magleby / Nordenbro / Snøde / Tved)
- `file-jsa.file` — Line 6 (Kresten, Langø household, emigrants, Wilhelm Krogh, Sophus' possible fourth daughter)
- `file-m4r.file` — Line 7 (South-West Jutland, Nybøl, Næstved, Malmö)

The files describe themselves as segments 1–7 of 8. Segment 8 (open questions, method and import checklist) was not present in the directory at the time of review. The overview claims 41 new people and four generations above the existing von Kleist line, but the person-level proposals have mixed confidence and must not be imported as a block.

## Overall assessment

This is a useful research-extension package, especially because it ties proposed changes to existing GEDCOM IDs, states confidence grades, identifies source codes, and separates new people from document schedules. The strongest material is the named archival-record work in the Gaardsted and Vibygård sections, the direct source conflict on Ove Samuel Gedde, and the explicit warning not to splice the two von Kleist reconstructions.

However, some entries marked A or B are more confident than the evidence currently warrants. In particular, an index extract is not the original probate, and a relationship inferred from an ambiguous index clause should not be imported as proven parentage. Treat the extension as a research plan and proposed patch, not an approved GEDCOM update.

## Critical corrections and cautions

### 1. Gaardsted: sibling clue strong; Tohde parentage not yet established

The G302 index entry for Niels Jensen Degn's probate (14 May 1787, no. 11, fol. 39) names Karen Christensdatter and describes Claus Gaardsted as her brother and tenant farmer at Vosnæsgård. It also refers to Christen Christensen Tohde, foged in Dejret, with a 1764 probate.

This is a strong lead for Karen and Claus as siblings. But the index's syntax — especially “afdødes stedbørn efter Christen Christensen Tohde? ... til datter Karen Christensdatter” — must be resolved against the original probate and the 1764 file. It does not yet safely establish that Tohde was the biological father of both Karen and Claus. The question mark attached to “Tohde” also means the surname transcription itself is uncertain.

Do not yet add Christen Christensen Tohde as father of Claus, change Claus's name to Claus Christensen, or assign a FAMC. First inspect the 1787 original and locate the 1764 probate. Also search Claus's own probate and the 1811 Bogensholm auction as the extension proposes.

Source index: https://slaegt.dk/media/jnnnvuml/alfabetisk-navneliste-og-skifteuddrag-til-rols%C3%B8g%C3%A5rd-gods-g302-mols-herred-1778-1816-1818.pdf
Secondary transcription: https://www.milloup.dk/hvh/download/skifter/rolsoegaard_gods_skifter.htm

### 2. Gedde: Ove Samuel's 1778/1788 conflict is real and significant

The new Gedde segment identifies the central issue correctly: the current GEDCOM has Ove Samuel born 28 February 1778, while Morten Clausen's genealogy gives 28 May 1788 in Fredrikstad and Anker's 1885 biography gives 28 May 1788. Harbou's 1897 article, “Slægten von Kleist i Danmark,” identifies Frederikke Louise Dorthea von Kleist's husband as Ove Samuel Gedde, born 28 February 1778 and died 10 March 1843. Anker identifies Hans Christopher Gedde as father of Ove Samuel, gives the 28 May 1788 birth, and names the two marriages of Hans Christopher.

Thus two substantial published genealogical sources identify the same Ove Samuel as the husband of Frederikke Louise and give different birth dates. This is not resolved by simply preferring one secondary source. The “two men merged” possibility raised by the extension is a legitimate hypothesis, but not proven by the ordinal in an online genealogy alone.

Sources:
- Anker 1885: https://runeberg.org/acj330bio/0110.html
- Harbou 1897: https://v-kleist.com/FG_allg/Kleist_in_Daenemark.pdf
- Online genealogy lead: http://mortenclausen.dk/GEDHT/fam155xx/fam15556.htm

**Next decisive work:** inspect the original 1778 and 1788 baptism candidates, the 1806 marriage, and the 1843 Helsingør burial/death record. Keep both dates and the possible two-person hypothesis in the research notes until identity is resolved. The new file's confidence grade for Hans Christopher as father is too high if based only on Anker/Geni; it is a strong published-source claim, not a primary-record confirmation.

### 3. von Kleist: excellent documentary roadmap, but two chains must remain separate

The Damen volume (2025) explicitly identifies Dubislaff/Bratta as son of Henning (I.M.8) and Margarethe von Wedelstädt, names five sons in the 13 April 1477 collective enfeoffment, and states that the sons were enfeoffed in 1486 after their father's death. This is strong family-history evidence and gives specific charter references (125 and 192) for follow-up.

The extension appropriately warns that Chain A (the numbered Muttrin-Damen family-history line) and Chain B (Geneanet's Prissebur reconstruction) must not be spliced. The many Kameke/Borcke relationships are marked C and derive from Geneanet; they should remain hypotheses, not imported parentage.

One correction in the extension's C-1 should not be applied as written: the Muttrin family-history text available in the 2012 edition explicitly says Prissebur appears in Urkunde 83 of 1364 as “Prißebur, der Klest heißt von Muttrin.” The Damen volume separately says Jarislav brought Damen to the family around 1365. These are different statements. Keep the 1364 Muttrin documentary reference distinct from the c.1365 Damen acquisition. Recheck the precise 2025 Muttrin edition before editing the website's note.

Sources:
- 2025 Damen volume: https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Muttrin/Damen.pdf
- Muttrin volume text (2012 edition): https://www.yumpu.com/de/document/view/7707886/geschlechts-v-kleist-familienverband-derer-v-kleist-ev
- Harbou 1897: https://v-kleist.com/FG_allg/Kleist_in_Daenemark.pdf

### 4. Syv / Vibygård: the 1820 probate extract is valuable; identity remains C

The extension quotes a Vibygaard probate extract dated 31 December 1820 for Maren Poulsdatter, wife of Hans Jensen, gardener, naming four adult children: Poul, Jens, Peder and Ane. The occupational and geographic convergence with the existing Syv-branch Hans Jensen is compelling.

But the extension itself correctly says the identification is C until the 1822 Syv marriage entry confirms that the groom was a widower after Maren Poulsdatter. Do not yet add the four children, Maren, or a first FAM to the canonical GEDCOM. Inspect the cited Vibygaard probate leaves and the 1822 marriage entry first.

Source transcription: https://aurelia-clemons.dk/vibygdx.htm

### 5. Rosmus: preserve the two Mette candidates

The extension sets out Mette Pedersdatter Hallis and Mette Sørensdatter Helles as competing readings for the 6 July 1811 marriage. It recommends reading the original marriage entry and not changing the tree based on the tertiary GEDCOM extract. This is the right approach. Keep both candidates and the conflict visible until the original Rosmus register is read.

### 6. Kresten / Marthe: good evidence plan; no resolution yet

The extension preserves two possible Marthe Jørgensen identities and gives a sensible order of tests: full 1877 baptism entry, 1878 burial of candidate B, Magleby tilgangsliste, and later census/children checks. The foster-parent evidence favours candidate B as a hypothesis but does not prove maternity. Keep the mother unresolved.

### 7. Langeland, emigration, Malmö, Nybøl and other branches

Lines 5–7 are primarily document schedules rather than newly proven ancestry. They are valuable because they name the specific registers, date ranges, existing GEDCOM IDs and reasons to inspect them. Preserve them as open leads, not as new relationships.

Particularly useful searches:
- 1787 Nordenbro probate after Christian Hansen: possible sibling group for Christian Hansen who married in 1829, not a demonstrated link.
- Fangel, Snøde, Magleby, Humble, Tved and Rudkøbing church books: named register targets.
- Jørgen Christensen and Bodil Margrethe's nine children: seven reportedly emigrated; individual protocol entries and overseas records still needed.
- Iver Peder Holgersen (Fresno 1910), Olaf Gedde (Shanghai 1904), Emil Gedde (Argentina 1922): preserve existing protocol details and follow destination records.
- Wilhelm Krogh: resolve “Tastrup” vs “Vastrup” from the original marginal note before selecting a German locality/Standesamt.
- Sophus Petersen's fourth daughter: family report only; search Nybøl beyond the three located entries before assuming a fourth birth.
- Lovisa Larsson: Swedish parish and household examination records are the correct next route.
- Assing, Hoven, Sønder Felding, Nybøl and Næstved: good primary-register worklist.

The recommendation to scan and preserve the family-held “Dødsliste for Sydlangeland 1900–1970” and Langø memoir is also worth retaining as a source-preservation task.

## Recommended work order

1. **Ove Samuel identity/date:** original baptism candidates (1778 and 1788), 1806 marriage, 1843 burial.
2. **Gaardsted:** original G302 probate fol. 39 and the 1764 Tohde probate; then Claus's own probate/1811 auction.
3. **Vibygård:** 1822 Syv marriage entry and original 1820 Vibygaard probate.
4. **von Kleist:** reconcile Chain A with the numbered 2025 family-history references; inspect charter 125 (1477) and 192 (1486); keep Geneanet Chain B separate.
5. **Rosmus and Kresten:** inspect the exact original church-book entries.
6. **Emigration and regional register schedules:** work by parish and contract number, recording negative results.

## Import policy

Do not apply the extension's “41 persons” as a batch. For each proposed person or relationship, require:
- exact existing GEDCOM ID (where applicable);
- source and page/folio/image reference;
- a transcription or faithful abstract;
- relationship type (biological, legal, social, step, foster, guardian, spouse);
- confidence grade and reason;
- conflict/negative-search note;
- proof that the person is not already represented under another ID.

The extension's A/B/C/D labels are useful but should be recalibrated when a claim relies on an index extract, a tertiary tree, or an unverified transcription. No canonical data was changed in this review.

# Archived file: leads/review-second-extension-batch-2026-11.md

# Review of second research-extension batch — 2026-11

Reviewed the six newly added files in `leads/`:

- `file-4wf.file` — Segment 12, Gedde second pass
- `file-9t3.file` — Segment 7, South-West Jutland / Nybøl / Næstved / Malmö
- `file-m49.file` — Segment 8, consolidated open questions, DNA method and import checklist
- `file-mbf.file` — Segment 10, index
- `file-xbh.file` — Segment 11, GEDCOM patch
- `file-yqa.file` — Segment 9, remaining frontiers

The new batch fills the previously missing Segment 8 and adds an index, a patch, a further Gedde pass and two additional document schedules. The six files have been retained unchanged. This review does not alter canonical people, relationships, or GEDCOM data.

## Overall assessment

This is a substantial and useful research plan. It distinguishes document schedules from ready-to-enter people, gives many exact parish/archive targets, and consolidates 49 open items (O-1–O-49). The main risk is that the patch and some confidence labels promote inferences beyond what the cited derivative extracts establish. Treat the patch as a proposal, not an executable import.

## High-priority cautions

### 1. Gaardsted / Rolsøgård — do not apply the structural patch as written

The patch's Section A creates Christen Christensen Tohde as the definite father of both Karen Christensdatter and existing person Claus Gaardsted; it assigns Claus the name `Claus Christensen` and adds FAMC/FAM136.

That exceeds the evidence currently transcribed. The 1787 G302 index extract is an entry in Niels Jensen Degn's probate, not Karen's probate. Its wording appears to mention inheritance to the deceased's stepchildren after Christen Christensen Tohde (question-marked/uncertain reading), a daughter Karen Christensdatter, and her brother Claus Gaardsted acting as lavværge. This is a strong sibling/kinship lead, but the syntax does not securely establish that Tohde was the biological or legal father of both, nor that Claus used Christensen as a patronymic. The 1764 probate itself and the full 1787 probate folio are still required.

**Recommendation:** do not apply F136, the father-child links, or the name change to `p72`. Keep the sibling clue and possible Tohde connection in notes with explicit uncertainty until the original records are read. This is consistent with the earlier review in [review-new-extension-files-2026-11.md](review-new-extension-files-2026-11.md).

### 2. Ove Samuel Gedde — two-person hypothesis is plausible, not yet proved

Segment 12 proposes:
- Ove Samuel I, born 28 February 1778, died young; and
- Ove Samuel II, born 28 May 1788 in Fredrikstad, died 10 March 1843 in Helsingør.

The evidence it cites is the ordinal in a compiled genealogy, a separate modern genealogy profile, Harbou's 1778 birth attached to the man who married in 1806 and died in 1843, and a biographical argument from Hans Christopher Gedde's Norwegian postings. This is a coherent explanation of the 1778/1788 conflict, but the ordinal and compiled profiles are not independent proof of two people. The posting argument is circumstantial. A birth in 1788 would make the 1806 groom about 18; possible, but not decisive.

**Recommendation:** retain the two-person hypothesis as a prominent research lead. Do not create Ove Samuel I or change `p216` to 1788/Fredrikstad until the 1778 burial and 1788 Fredrikstad baptism have been checked. Segment 12's own O-46 and O-47 are the right next steps. Also inspect Harbou's full treatment before attributing the conflation to Harbou as a demonstrated error.

### 3. Fischer sisters — preserve the correction as a sourced proposal, not yet primary fact

The batch proposes Frederikke Christiane Fischer (1745–1787) as Hans Christopher Gedde's first wife and Øllegaard Sophie Fischer (1752–1821) as his second. The supplied support is a genealogy quoting a family account and a Wikipedia-derived reference in the patch. This may resolve the wife-order problem, but it is not equivalent to locating the marriages, burials, or a reliable contemporary family record.

**Recommendation:** retain both candidate identities and the proposed order in the research notes; verify against the full Harbou text and primary records before structural changes. Do not silently change `p433` from first to second wife as if primary-verified.

### 4. Lorenz's paternity and DNA plan — keep relationship categories separate

Segment 8 correctly wants all three paternal roles recorded, but its wording needs care:
- Sophus Petersen: family account identifies him as biological father.
- Wilhelm Krogh: the 1932 civil-register marginal note/court process records legal acknowledgement/registered paternity; this is not, by itself, genetic proof.
- Peter Frederiksen: social/stepfather and surname-giver, according to the family account.

Do not label Krogh “biological per the register” as a proven biological fact. A legal paternity ruling and a DNA conclusion are different kinds of evidence. Likewise, the claim that Peter was paid to marry Else should remain attributed to its source unless independently documented.

The proposed MyHeritage shared-match subtraction can help form candidate clusters, but the stated cM bands and the assertion that matches below 30 cM are “noise” are too categorical. Shared-match lists alone do not identify a father; relationship ranges overlap, and small matches are not automatically irrelevant. Use documented match amounts, relationship hypotheses, triangulation/segment information where available, known relatives on the Frederiksen side, and independent documentary evidence. Treat the proposed descendant test as useful, not as an automatic “closes it outright” result.

### 5. Vibygård / Syv patch — wait for the 1822 marriage

The 1820 Vibygaard probate extract may identify Maren Poulsdatter's adult children, including a gartner named Jens/Hans Jensen (as transcribed in the source files). The patch then attaches the four children to existing `a66` and adds the 1820 Maren marriage as his first marriage. The 1822 Syv marriage is still listed as open (O-11). The shared occupation and locality are a good identity clue, but the patch itself acknowledges that the marriage entry has not confirmed the match.

**Recommendation:** do not add F137/F138 or the four child links until the 1822 marriage and original probate wording establish the identity and relationships. Keep the named probate children as provisional individuals/leads, not confirmed children of `a66`.

### 6. Count and patch consistency

Segment 8 says there are 11 new persons in clear GEDCOM-ready form. Segment 10's “New persons — GEDCOM-ready” list actually enumerates seven individual IDs (Christen, Maren, four children, Willum), while Section A of the patch also creates Karen. The count/list/patch do not reconcile cleanly. The patch also contains proposed relationships that are not ready for import, as noted above.

**Recommendation:** reconcile the count and inventory by unique person ID before any import. Check every proposed ID against the existing data and the duplicate-ID issue already documented for `p13`.

## Useful new research routes to preserve

- **Assing / Jens Peder Larsen:** the leap-day 29 February 1812 clue is worth following, but the parents remain unverified until the Assing birth/baptism register is inspected.
- **Hoven mill household:** the fixed mill property, widow/owner census entries and aftægts household clues make this a practical archival search.
- **Nybøl / Paakjær:** the distinction between church registers and post-1867 German Standesamt records is important. Verify each generation through the appropriate register; the farm's 1879 building and fire-insurance records are promising collateral sources.
- **Malmö / Lovisa Larsson:** Malmö birth and household registers, then the 1885 migration records, are a sound route to identify her mother and trace Niels Larsen.
- **Fuglslev / Rousing:** the file itself correctly marks the upper generations as name-pattern/household hypotheses. The herred appointment, 1787/1801 censuses and parish records should be read before extending the line.
- **Niels Jørgensen:** resolve the ambiguous “Hove/Høje” wording from the full 1854 marriage entry before selecting a baptism.
- **Herfølge/Herlufmagle and Broager/Nybøl branches:** the proposed marriage and parish register sequence is actionable; preserve the distinction between documented fosterage and biological parentage.
- **Family-held Langeland documents:** scanning and preserving the one-copy dødsliste and memoir is a worthwhile source-preservation priority.

## Recommended order of work

1. Read the full Harbou text and reconcile each citation, especially the Gedde and von Kleist entries.
2. Obtain the original 1764 Tohde probate and full 1787 G302 folio before deciding Claus/Karen parentage.
3. Check Ove Samuel's 1778 burial and the 1788 Fredrikstad baptism.
4. Check the 1822 Syv marriage and full 1820 Vibygaard probate before linking Maren and the four children to `a66`.
5. Verify the Fischer marriages/deaths from contemporary records.
6. Continue the document schedules in Segments 7 and 9, recording unsuccessful searches as well as positive results.
7. Only then prepare a corrected patch, with each person and relationship separately sourced and typed (biological, legal, social/step, foster/guardian, household).

## Import decision

**Do not apply `file-xbh.file` wholesale.** In particular, hold the Gaardsted structural changes, the Viby/Syv family links, and the Ove Samuel decision. Notes that preserve conflicts can be added only if clearly labelled as reported claims and hypotheses, without changing relationship structure. No canonical data or GEDCOM has been changed by this review.



---

# Archived file: leads/review-final-extension-batch-2026-11.md

# Review of final research-extension batch — 2026-11

Reviewed the three newly added files:

- `file-up7.file` — Segment 13, Harbou reconciliation worksheet
- `file-m4d.file` — Segment 14, negatives log
- `file-dk8.file` — Segment 15, closing assessment

Together with Segments 1–12 already reviewed, these complete the extension's stated 15-segment sequence. The new files are retained unchanged. This review does not edit canonical people, relationships, or GEDCOM data.

## What the new batch contributes

### Segment 13 — Harbou reconciliation worksheet

The worksheet identifies 29 PHT:1897 citations in the GEDCOM and gives a practical row-by-row process:
- find Harbou's own numbering for each person;
- transcribe what the Danish and German versions actually say;
- classify each citation as CONFIRM, CORRECT, HARBOU ONLY, or SILENT;
- retain or qualify citations according to the result rather than deleting them prematurely.

This is a strong audit method. The special checks are well chosen: Esther von Kameke, Anna von Fürst/Fürsten, Hedvig Maria von Kleist, and Cartz Ulrik's military service. The distinction in Section E is also important: Harbou may be a valuable contemporary secondary authority for the Danish generations he knew, but is not primary evidence for medieval/Pomeranian ancestry.

**Caution:** the worksheet is a blank audit instrument, not the reconciliation itself. Its existence does not verify any of the 29 citations. The instruction to read both language versions and record differences should be followed before changing citations or person data.

### Segment 14 — negatives log

The log records the search phrase, scope, null result, meaning and retry route. This is the right structure for negative evidence. Especially useful:
- the emigrant web searches are distinguished from the Danish emigration protocol entries already transcribed;
- the Syv 1822 marriage search is correctly treated as a web-indexing failure, not evidence that the marriage did not occur;
- the Tohde name remains explicitly uncertain;
- the G305 search is separated from the Rolsøgård G302 series;
- timeouts, CAPTCHA and scrape errors are correctly distinguished from a record not existing;
- the “Hove/Høje/Høve” issue is recorded as an unresolved place-name reading, not silently normalized.

**Caution:** some statements in the log are conclusions about catalogue coverage or archive structure. Preserve them as the researcher's reported search results until the catalogue/series descriptions and original registers are independently checked. A negative search is evidence only for the exact series, dates, indexes, spelling variants and access conditions actually searched.

The log's own retry list is useful. In particular, a failure to retrieve a web page or a timeout is not a negative genealogical result. The Geni profile and the Muttrin PDF are explicitly retryable technical failures.

### Segment 15 — closing assessment

The closing summary is a useful statement of the extension author's priorities and confidence judgements. It emphasizes two claimed documentary gains (Gaardsted and Vibygård), the proposed Fischer correction, the Ove Samuel split, and the need to hold back the large von Kleist extension.

However, its import recommendations need to be read alongside the actual source transcriptions and earlier review, not treated as a new source in themselves.

## Material disagreements and import cautions

### 1. Claus Gaardsted's father — A-grade claim remains unverified here

Segment 15 says one clause in a 1787 probate names Claus's father, office, parish and probate year, and calls the finding A-grade. But the underlying transcription previously supplied in the extension says, in substance, that inheritance in Niels Jensen Degn's estate concerned the deceased's stepchildren after Christen Christensen Tohde (surname itself marked with a question mark), including daughter Karen Christensdatter, with brother Claus Gaardsted acting as lavværge.

That wording strongly suggests a family connection and makes the 1764 probate an excellent target. It does not, without the original folio and careful reading of the full sentence, safely establish that Christen Christensen Tohde was Claus's father or that Claus's surname/patronymic should become Christensen. Segment 14 itself reinforces the uncertainty: the name Tohde has no independent online occurrence and the transcriber marked it uncertain.

**Decision:** preserve the new A-grade assertion as the extension author's conclusion, but do not apply the father-child relationship, `Claus Christensen` name change, or FAMC until the original 1787 folio and the 1764 probate have been examined. The new negatives log does not resolve the underlying syntax.

### 2. Vibygård probate — primary record does not alone establish identity with `a66`

Segment 15 says the 1820 probate has four adults, occupations and a husband, and claims four-way convergence on the identification with the existing Syv/Viby person. Segment 14 still says the 1822 Syv marriage was not found online and must be checked page-by-page. The earlier patch proposal itself treats the 1822 marriage as an outstanding check.

A probate may securely establish the named relationships within the deceased's own household. The further step—identifying the named husband/guardian with existing `a66`—is a separate identity resolution. “Four-way convergence” needs to be unpacked into the actual independent records and matching details before it is accepted.

**Decision:** preserve the four probate-named people and their reported relationships as a high-priority lead. Do not attach them to `a66` or create the proposed family structure until the probate original and the 1822 Syv marriage (or equally strong independent identity evidence) have been read.

### 3. Fischer sisters and Hans Christopher Gedde

Segment 15 calls Hans Christopher as Ove Samuel's father and the Fischer sisters B-grade, citing an encyclopaedia and a family account. This is useful secondary evidence and may justify a carefully attributed note. It is not equivalent to primary marriage/death evidence. Segment 13's Harbou audit may improve the picture, but the worksheet is not yet completed.

**Decision:** retain the proposed wife order and full dates as sourced claims/hypotheses; do not silently overwrite the live family structure until the source passages are inspected and the conflict with existing evidence is documented.

### 4. Ove Samuel split

Segment 15 says the conflation is well-evidenced but that the correct birth date is unresolved. That is a fair summary of the hypothesis, but the 1778 burial and 1788 Fredrikstad baptism remain open. Do not create a second Ove Samuel or change `p216` to 1788 until those records are checked.

### 5. von Kleist extension

Segment 15 appropriately advises against importing the five-generation extension wholesale. Segment 13 gives the right next step: reconcile Harbou's numbering, identify the gaps, and only then test the Pomeranian generations against the family-history volumes and Greifswald charters. Keep the distinction between the 1364 Muttrin document reference and the c.1365 Damen acquisition; the earlier C-1 correction should not erase the 1364 reference where it belongs.

## Recommended next actions

1. Complete Segment 13's 29-row Harbou worksheet using both language versions; do not mark rows closed based on the worksheet template alone.
2. Obtain and read the original 1787 Rolsøgård probate folio and 1764 Christen Christensen Tohde probate before deciding Claus's father or patronymic.
3. Read the original 1820 Vibygård probate and the 1822 Syv marriage before linking its family to `a66`.
4. Verify the Fischer marriage order and dates against Harbou and primary records.
5. Locate Ove Samuel's 1778 burial and 1788 Fredrikstad baptism.
6. Retain the negatives log, including its exact query/scope and retry routes. Add new negative entries only when the scope is reproducible.
7. Scan and preserve the Sydlangeland dødsliste and Lisbeth Skov memoir as stated in the earlier segments.
8. Keep the von Kleist extension out of canonical data until the Harbou number reconciliation and primary Pomeranian checks are complete.

## Overall assessment

The 15-segment extension is a valuable research dossier and now has a coherent open-question list, source key, index, negatives log and proposed import workflow. Its strongest contribution is not the number of proposed people, but the explicit research route and the effort to preserve unresolved alternatives.

The closing summary's “ready to import” labels are the author's assessment, not independent verification. The most important unresolved risk remains promotion of a plausible identity or probate interpretation into a definite parent-child relationship. No canonical data should be changed until the underlying records are checked.

No canonical tree or GEDCOM records were changed in this review.

# Archived file: leads/von-kleist-source-audit-2026-11-16.md

# von Kleist source audit — 16 November 2026

## Scope

This audit covers the 23 proposed people exp020–exp042 in the expanded research JSON and their proposed connection to existing people p3472 (Pribislaff von Kleist) and p3473 (Esther von Kameke). It also checks a related Tychow-Dubberow branch because the two reconstructions may be connected through the 1477 fief letter.

This is a source audit, not an import decision. The original uploaded exports are preserved unchanged. No canonical tree relationship is changed by this note.

## Main sources inspected

1. **Muttrin-Damensche Linie — Muttrin**, Geschichte des Geschlechts v. Kleist, Third Part, Third Department, 2nd revised edition (Hamm, 2025), based on H. Kypke (1885) and additions by Hans Wätjen (1979), revised by Heinrich v. Kleist-Retzow and Sigurd v. Kleist:
   https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Muttrin/Muttrin.pdf
2. **Tychow-Dubberower Linie**, Geschichte des Geschlechts v. Kleist, Third Part, Second Department, 2nd revised edition (Hamm, 2025):
   https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Dubberow.pdf
3. **Allgemeine Geschichte**, 2nd edition (2007), including L. Quandt's analysis of the early generations and the 1477 collective fief letter:
   https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Genealogie/AllgemeineGeschichte.pdf
4. The family association's online name and spouse indexes, useful as finding aids, not independent proof:
   https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/NamenabfrageL.html
   https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/toechter.htm
   https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/ehefrauen.htm

The 2025 Muttrin volume explicitly explains that its early Stammtafel follows L. Quandt's 1871 arrangement, based on some contemporary documents but also much later family records. It calls the early arrangement a wahrscheinliche Anordnung (probable reconstruction). The volume also says that the old family-history texts have been reworked and that additions from archival records and other sources are marked separately. These qualifications must accompany the pedigree; the 2025 date of the edition does not make every early-generation claim a contemporary record.

## 1. Early Muttrin ancestry: what is actually supported

The Muttrin volume, printed pages 5–6 (accessible PDF text around lines 293–333), makes these distinctions:

- It reports that Prißebur, called “der Klest von Muttrin,” appears in Urkunde 83 of 1364 as a co-owner of land at Glineke.
- It says the names Jarßlaff and Henning as Prissebur's sons come from a family genealogy dated 1576 and an older letter, not from the 1364 wording alone.
- It identifies Henning M.8 as a son of Henning M.3, alive in 1435, and says his marriage to a von Wedelstett and seven sons are reported according to the old family genealogy.
- It identifies Bisprow III.1 as a son of Henning M.8 and refers to the 13 April 1477 fief letter; the biography then names his three sons Henning, Peter and Hans.

The 1364 occurrence and the reconstructed parent-child sequence are therefore different evidence propositions. The first is a dated documentary reference as reported by the 2025 edition; the second is a pedigree reconstruction using later family material.

## 2. Proposed people exp020–exp041: itemized audit

The table below reports what the supplied notes claim and what the source check currently permits. “Source-reported” means that the family-history volume states the relationship; it does not mean that I have independently inspected the underlying medieval charter or original parish/estate record.

| ID | Person / proposed role | Evidence assessment and outstanding issue |
|---|---|---|
| exp020 | Klest de Densin | The 2025 Muttrin volume places him at the head of Quandt's early Stammtafel and explicitly qualifies the arrangement as probable. The proposed 1289 identification and fatherhood of Prissebur need to be checked against Quandt's full argument and the cited charter. Keep as a reconstructed early ancestor, not a fully proven parent. |
| exp021 | Prissebur | The 1364 Urkunde 83 reference is specifically reported by the 2025 volume. The parent link to exp020 and sons Jarsslaff/Henning derive from the later pedigree/older letter as reported by that volume. Do not give all three claims the same evidentiary grade. |
| exp022 | Henning M.3 | The proposed son of Prissebur and father of Henning M.8 are reported through the 1576 genealogy and later reconstruction. The 1407 record is a separate dated reference to Henning M.8; it does not by itself prove all upstream parentage. |
| exp042 | Henning von Mutteryn (M.8) | The Muttrin volume identifies him as the younger son of Henning M.3, alive in 1435 (Urk.104), and says that according to the old family genealogy he married a von Wedelstett and had seven sons including Bisprow III.1. The structured record was missing from the agent export; it has now been added to the expanded JSON/GEDCOM as the intervening generation. Marriage and seven-son list remain attributed to old family genealogy. |
| exp038 | Unnamed von Wedelstett | The Muttrin volume says Henning M.8 was married to a von Wedelstett “according to the old family genealogy” and attributes seven sons to that union. Her identity and maternity remain dependent on that later genealogy. The structured spouse link has now been corrected to the missing Henning M.8 record exp042. |
| exp024 | Bisprow III.1 of Muttrin | The 2025 Muttrin text explicitly calls him son of Henning M.8 and refers to the 13 April 1477 fief letter; it also reports his three sons and his death by c.1493 from the 1493 church-patronage event. The expanded JSON/GEDCOM now link him to exp042 (Henning M.8), not directly to exp022 (Henning M.3). Strong source-reported genealogy, but check the charter and 1493 record before calling the parent link primary-verified. |
| exp025 | Unnamed von Natzmer | The Muttrin biography says Bisprow's wife was a born von Natzmer. The supplied data treats her as mother of his children; the source's family account supports the union, but her personal identity and exact maternity are not independently established here. |
| exp026 | Hans III.6 of Muttrin | The supplied note identifies him as Bisprow's youngest son and cites 1477, 1493 and 1498 records; it infers death in 1515/early 1516 from the sons' 1516 enfeoffment. This is a useful dated-record sequence. Exact wife/mother details still need the relevant biography and records. |
| exp027 | Unnamed von Bornentin | The proposed wife of Hans III.6 and daughter of Paul at Borntin is attributed to the family genealogy. Treat the marriage and parentage as source-reported, not independently proved. |
| exp028 | Pribislaff III.22 of Borntin | The JSON note attributes a substantial biography and the marriage to Dorothea Elisabeth von Below to the Muttrin family history. The family association's name index confirms that a Pribislaff III.22 is listed at Muttrin/Borntin and associates him with Dorothea Elisabeth von Below; this is a useful cross-reference, not independent proof. The exact current-edition page/number crosswalk remains to be recorded from the relevant biography. |
| exp029 | Dorothea Elisabeth von Below | The proposed parents Gerhard Henning von Below and Catharina von Glasenapp are quoted from the Muttrin genealogy. The relationship is a clear source claim; independent records for her parents have not been located in this pass. |
| exp039 | Gerhard Henning von Below | The supplied note identifies him as father of Dorothea Elisabeth. This is currently a genealogy-reported relationship; no independent biographical or primary record was checked. |
| exp040 | Catharina von Glasenapp | The supplied note identifies her as mother of Dorothea Elisabeth. This is currently a genealogy-reported relationship; no independent biographical or primary record was checked. |
| exp030 | Hans III.54 of Borntin | The supplied note identifies him as son of Pribislaff III.22 and husband of Elisabeth von Kleist of Ruschitz. The specific numbered biography and its cited fief records need to be matched to the 2025 edition before importing the link. |
| exp031 | Elisabeth von Kleist of Ruschitz | The note quotes the family-history claim that she was a daughter of Andreas of Ruschitz. Treat as a source-reported parentage claim; the original record and her mother's identity remain open. |
| exp041 | Andreas von Kleist of Ruschitz | The proposed father of Elisabeth is identified only through the marriage description in the family genealogy. No independent record for him was checked. |
| exp032 | Pribislaff III.114 of Muttrin and Borntin | The supplied note reports a detailed life with dated fief, church-patronage and baptismal-font references, and identifies him as son of Hans III.54 and father of Hans Jürgen III.186. These are promising documentary anchors; each cited document and the exact old/new numbering must still be reconciled. |
| exp033 | Hans Jürgen III.186 | The note gives a 1621 school-register entry, 1627 disputation, 1641 purchase and 1643 district office, and says he was son of Pribislaff III.114. It also says his son was Pribislaff III.281, existing p3472. The structured JSON does not encode p3472.father = exp033, so narrative and relationship fields disagree. The exact biography and parent-child wording need to be checked before adding the link. |
| exp034 | Unnamed daughter of Achatz | The note says she was the wife of Hans Jürgen and daughter of Landrat Achatz III.110. Her given name is not supplied. Do not invent one. |
| exp035 | Achatz von Kleist III.110 | The proposed father of exp034 is stated in the family genealogy. The person's identity and the exact numbered entry need a source-page crosswalk. |
| exp036 | Jacob von Kameke of Körlin | The source note quotes the Muttrin biography of Pribislaff III.281 as calling Anna Esther von Kameke a daughter of the electoral captain Jacob of Körlin. This is a clear source-reported relationship; his own identity and career need separate verification. |
| exp037 | Catharina von Koller | The same quoted passage names her as Anna Esther's mother and Jacob's wife. The relationship is source-reported; no independent record was checked. |

### Existing person p3472 and p3473

The expanded JSON updates the existing person p3472 with a note identifying him as Pribislaff of Muttrin, Döbel and Borntin, died 1698, and identifies his wife as Anna Esther von Kameke. The family association's indexes corroborate the old family-history numbering and spouse-name form:

- NamenabfrageL lists Pribislaff auf Muttrin, III.281, died 1698, wife Anna Esther von Kameke.
- The spouse index lists Anna Esther von Kameke as wife of Pribislaff III.281.
- The daughters index lists children under Pribislaff III.281.

These indexes support identity reconciliation between existing p3472 and the proposed III.281 person, and between existing p3473 (previously “Esther von Kameke”) and Anna Esther. They are derivative indexes to the family history, not independent proof. The expanded JSON gives p3473 parents exp036 and exp037, but the canonical data still has no parents for p3472 or p3473.

**Important remaining structural inconsistency:** exp033's note says his son Pribislaff III.281 is existing p3472, but the expanded JSON still leaves p3472.father null and does not set a father field on exp033 linking him to p3472. This must be reconciled from the actual numbered biography before the proposed father-child link is encoded in either JSON or GEDCOM.

**Corrected skipped generation:** The original agent export linked exp024 (Bisprow III.1) directly to exp022 (Henning M.3), and linked exp038 (the von Wedelstett wife) to exp022. That contradicted the source notes, which identify an intervening Henning M.8. The expanded research package now contains exp042 (Henning M.8), with exp022 → exp042 → exp024 and exp038 married to exp042. This correction was applied to the expanded JSON, expanded GEDCOM, full text export, and the concise expanded text summary. The expanded package now contains 524 people (489 site records plus 35 research additions) and 160 families. The canonical site data/GEDCOM was not changed.

## 3. A material conflict in the early Tychow ancestry

The earlier review of the expanded Tychow text was too definite when it presented Hans I.24 as the established father of Reimer II.2 and Bartes II.3. The family-history sources contain a real conflict that must be preserved.

### What the 1477 charter is transcribed as saying

L. Quandt's Allgemeine Geschichte, in its discussion/transcription of the 13 April 1477 collective fief letter, lists “Pribbeslaw zu Muttrin mit seinen Söhnen, die Brüder Reymer und Bartes zu [Groß-]Tichow”. The accessible text is around line 1042 of:
https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Genealogie/AllgemeineGeschichte.pdf

Read literally, this identifies Reimer and Bartes as sons of Pribbeslaw of Muttrin. The original charter or its authenticated copy should be checked before treating the transcription as definitive.

### What the 2025 Tychow-Dubberow biography says

The 2025 Tychow-Dubberow volume introduces Reimer and Bartes as the two sons of Hans of Tychow (I.24), then gives Reimer's son Bartez II.6 and Bartes's sons Martin II.9, Jacob II.10 and Andreas II.11:
https://www.v-kleist.com/FG/Dubberow.pdf, accessible lines 5616–5647 and 6021–6055.

This is a later family-history reconstruction. It conflicts with the literal wording of the 1477 charter as transcribed in Allgemeine Geschichte.

### What the general family history says about the uncertainty

In its reproduction/discussion of the old family genealogy, Allgemeine Geschichte says that the name of the Tychow/Dubberow branch's earlier brother/ancestor is not known from the family records (accessible lines 1373 and footnote 169 around line 1390). It also describes how later family genealogies assigned the three branches to named ancestors. This is another reason not to silently treat Hans I.24 as a contemporary-record fact.

### What remains secure within the Tychow line

The 2025 Tychow-Dubberow volume explicitly distinguishes the two sons/branches:

- Reimer II.2's son is Bartez II.6.
- Bartes II.3's sons include Andreas (Drews) II.11.
- Andreas II.11 is father of Carsten II.21.
- Carsten II.21 is father of Bernd II.39.
- Bernd II.39 is father of Christian II.60.

Thus the expanded text's chain Reimer II.2 → Bartez II.6 → Andreas II.11 is still wrong: Bartez II.6 is not Andreas's father. But the upstream father of Reimer and Bartes must be recorded as disputed between the 1477 charter transcription (Pribbeslaw of Muttrin) and the later Tychow genealogy (Hans I.24), pending examination of the underlying documents and the genealogy's reasoning.

This matters to the broader project because the Muttrin and Tychow lines may be connected through this disputed 1477 parentage. Do not merge their reconstructions or treat the proposed exp020–exp041 Muttrin chain as automatically proving the Tychow branch's parentage.

## 4. Current decision and work plan

- Keep exp020–exp042 as research additions, not a batch import.
- Preserve the 1364 Prissebur reference separately from the reconstructed parentage around him.
- Preserve the 1477 Reimer/Bartes father conflict; do not encode Hans I.24 or Pribbeslaw as their canonical father yet.
- Correct the Tychow tree diagram so Bartez II.6 is a collateral branch, not the father of Andreas II.11.
- Reconcile the 2025 edition's numbering with the older III. numbers used in the JSON and Harbou; do not assume they are interchangeable.
- Locate the exact Muttrin biography for III.281 / existing p3472 and verify whether it names Hans Jürgen III.186 as father.
- Verify the Jacob von Kameke / Catharina von Koller parentage claim and the exp034/exp035 Vietzow connection against the relevant biographies and any original records cited there.

No canonical people or relationships have been changed by this audit.

# Archived file: leads/family-tree-image-catalog.md

# Image Catalog — Slægten Gårdsted og Gedde

**Date:** November 2026
**Scope:** Portraits, photos, paintings, and contextual images (castles, estates, documents) for ancestors in the expanded family tree at `family-tree-expanded.txt`.
**Format:** Each entry includes the person's name, dates, the type of image, the source URL, and a license note where applicable.

## How to use

Click any URL to view the image in your browser. Most Wikimedia Commons images are in the public domain (PD-expired, mostly because the artist died >100 years ago). Finn Holbek's portraits are © Finn Holbek / finnholbek.dk, used here for reference. WikiTree and Geni images have varying licenses — see each source page for details.

---
